What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, thank you for seeing me. Um... well, what brings me in is... it's my work, but it's not just my work, it's my head. I'm an information systems manager, I've been doing this a long time, got my master's degree, so I'm very used to complex environments. But it's gotten to the point where I keep getting these thoughts. Just these constant, heavy thoughts that if I miss one setting—just one tiny configuration on the servers—or if I overlook one access rule in the security protocols, or even just miss a subtle warning sign in the system logs... someone could get seriously hurt. \n\nOr, you know, people could lose everything. The company could go under, people could lose their livelihoods, their data, everything. And the frustrating thing is, part of me knows it’s irrational. I know we have redundancies built in, I know how the architecture works, I'm an educated guy. But it doesn't matter what the logical side of my brain says, because the thought just hits me so hard, like I’m personally responsible for every catastrophic thing that could possibly happen. I feel the weight of it like it's an absolute certainty. \n\nAnd I can’t just move on from it. I mean, most people would just close their laptop at five o'clock and go home to their families, right? But I can't do that. I just get stuck on that thought, over and over, playing out the disaster in my head, thinking about how it would all be my fault because of one missed line of code or one access rule, and it's just... it's exhausting. It's taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these thoughts about something going wrong at work come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yes. One hundred percent against my will. I mean, it's exactly like I was just saying... I don't sit down and choose to think about missing an access rule. I don't want to think about overlooking a warning sign in the system logs or missing a server setting. Who would want to think about that, right? \n\nBut it just... it just invades my brain. I'll be driving home, or maybe trying to sit down and watch a movie, and suddenly—bam. The thought just forces its way in. I'll try to push it away, I'll tell myself, you know, \"Hey, you know the architecture, you checked the redundancies, it's completely irrational to think this way.\" I try so hard to just push it out. But it doesn't matter what I want. The thought just parks itself right there in the front of my mind, and it brings this heavy, crushing feeling that someone is going to get hurt, or the company is going to lose everything, and it's going to be all my fault. \n\nIt's like my brain is hijacking itself. I just want to move on, you know? I want to just eat dinner or watch the game like a normal person, but I can't, because the thought just won't leave, no matter how hard I try to force it out or distract myself. It's completely without my permission, if that makes sense. It just... it takes over, and I'm just stuck there fighting with my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly doing anything or checking something in an attempt to make these thoughts go away or prevent the disasters you're imagining?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I do a lot of things. It's... well, it's embarrassing, to be honest, but since we're talking about it, I guess I should just lay it all out there. \n\nWhen those thoughts hit, the ones where I'm convinced I missed a setting and everything is going to crash, I can't just sit with it. I have to do something. So, I'll go back and reread the deployment plans. And I don't mean just skimming them to double-check a date or a version number. I mean I read them line by line, over and over again. I'll read the same paragraph five, six, seven times. And it's not just the plans... it's the audit logs, too. I'll scroll through lines of code and log entries, looking for that one tiny error I'm convinced I made. Even old messages—emails, Slack messages to my team from weeks ago—I'll dig them up and read them repeatedly to make sure I didn't give a wrong instruction that could lead to a catastrophe. \n\nAnd while I'm doing this, I... I have this counting thing. I'll count through the steps of the deployment or the security checks in my head. One, two, three, four... and it has to land on a \"safe\" number, or it has to feel a certain way. If I get distracted, or if the feeling isn't right—if it doesn't feel \"complete\" or \"safe\"—I have to start the whole counting process over. And I have to reread the document again. \n\nIt gets so bad, doctor, I've... I've stayed at the office for hours after everyone else has gone home. The lights are off in the rest of the building, the cleaning crew has come and gone, and I'm just sitting there at my desk, locked in this cycle. Because if I try to stop before that \"right\" feeling hits, if I try to just pack up my bag and walk out the door, the panic is unbearable. It physically hurts in my chest. \n\nAnd it's not even just the computer stuff anymore. It's bleeding into other things. When I finally do leave the office, I'll check the door locks. I'll lock the door, pull the handle, walk away, and then the thought hits—\"Did it actually latch? What if someone gets in and accesses the server room?\" So I go back. I pull the handle again. I'll do it ten, twenty times. I'll count the pulls. It's just... it's a nightmare. I'm spending hours doing this stuff just to make the thoughts stop, and it barely even works for more than a few minutes before the cycle starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking or counting behaviors to relieve the anxiety caused by your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That is exactly it. It’s entirely about the anxiety. I mean, like I was just saying a minute ago about rereading the deployment plans and the audit logs and all those old messages... I’m not doing any of that because I’m just trying to be a diligent manager, you know? I’m doing it because the anxiety is so intense, it’s like a physical weight sitting right on my chest. \n\nWhen that thought hits me—that I missed something, that the network is vulnerable and it’s going to ruin everything—my heart just starts pounding, my stomach drops, and the only way to make it stop, the only way to get even a tiny ounce of relief, is to do the checking. So I’ll sit there, and I’ll read the logs over and over, or I’ll be counting through the deployment steps in my head, just desperately praying to hit that 'safe' feeling. Because until I hit that feeling, the anxiety just keeps escalating. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same thing with the door locks. That’s exactly why I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves. It’s not because there’s actual work to do, or because I want to be there. God, I want to be home. But I’m trapped trying to get that anxiety to just drop, even a little bit. If I try to stop before it feels complete, before I get that exact right 'safe' feeling... the panic is just completely unbearable. It's like I'm suffocating. I can't even get to my car. I’d sit there staring at the approval forms, and the panic would just take over, and I’d have to go back and check the redundancy protocols again, and then read the logs, and count the steps... well, you know, everything I just told you about. I couldn't bring myself to just put my name on it and say \"go.\" \n\nAnd because of that, my team... God, it’s humiliating, honestly. My own team has just started working around me. The people I’m supposed to be leading! They don't rely on me anymore. They’ll go to other directors or just quietly make the calls themselves because they know if they bring a decision to my desk, it’s going to get swallowed up in my endless checking loops. It makes me feel completely useless, like I'm just a ghost in my own department. \n\nAnd then, you know, you asked about outside of work... it's bleeding into all of that, too. I used to have a life. I really did. I had these routines that kept me grounded, kept me feeling like a normal, functioning human being. Like, I used to play in this pickup basketball game, and I’d go to these salsa dancing nights on the weekends. Which, I know, a 43-year-old IT guy doing salsa, it sounds a little funny maybe, but I loved it. It was my release. The music, the people, just moving and not thinking about servers or deployment schedules. \n\nBut I’ve just... I’ve completely stopped showing up. To all of it. I mean, how can I go play basketball when I’m stuck at the office for three extra hours yanking on a door handle? Or how can I be present and enjoy a dance when my brain is screaming at me that the whole company network is going to collapse and I'm going to be the one blamed for it? I can't. So I just stay home, or I stay at the office, and... I'm losing all of it. I'm losing the people I used to see, I'm losing the routines that kept me sane... I just don't even know who I am outside of this constant panic anymore. It just takes up every inch of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these counting or checking behaviors are necessary to neutralize or undo the thoughts that are disturbing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, it is a lot like what I was just trying to explain a minute ago, but it really is about... undoing it. It is like the thought itself is a virus, you know? Being an IT guy, I guess that is how I picture it. The thought pops into my head that I missed a security flaw, or that someone is going to breach the server room, and it literally feels like the disaster has already been set in motion just because the thought occurred to me. \n\nSo, yeah, when I am sitting there rereading those deployment plans, or combing through the audit logs and those old messages for the hundredth time... I am actively trying to neutralize that danger. I am trying to erase it from reality. And the counting... the counting is a huge part of that neutralizing process. Like I said before, I will be counting through the steps in my head, and I have to reach that safe feeling. Because if a bad thought about the network crashing spikes while I am on step three, then step three is ruined. It is contaminated by the thought. So I have to start the whole thing over, and I have to keep counting and rereading until the thought is gone and the entire process feels completely safe. \n\nIt is the exact same mechanism with the door locks. I am pulling on that handle over and over to physically undo the image in my head of an intruder walking right in and destroying our hardware. And if I do not get it to feel complete... well, that is exactly why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. I literally cannot stop. I have had the night security guard ask me if I am okay because I am just standing there in a dark hallway, sweating, yanking a door handle or sitting at my desk staring at a screen. But if I try to force myself to just stop before it feels complete, before the thought is actually neutralized... the panic is so absolute, it feels like I am intentionally choosing to let the company be destroyed. I just cannot walk away with that on my conscience, so I keep checking and counting until the thought is undone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about work and the potential disasters are a part of yourself or are they something that is being imposed on you from the outside?\n\nPatient: No, it’s... I know they’re my thoughts. I mean, I don't think anyone is beaming them into my head or anything like that, if that's what you're asking. They are definitely coming from my own brain. But at the same time, it almost feels like my brain has been hijacked by this worst-case-scenario generator that I can't turn off. \n\nHonestly, that’s really what brings me in here to see you. I just keep getting these thoughts, these relentless, looping thoughts, that if I miss just one single setting on a server configuration, or if I overlook one tiny access rule in the firewall, or if I brush past one little warning sign in the system diagnostics... that the whole thing is going to come crashing down. And it goes beyond just getting fired or the company losing money. My mind takes it to the absolute extreme, telling me that because of my mistake, someone could actually get hurt, or our clients could lose everything they've built because their data was compromised. \n\nAnd the most frustrating part of all of this—the part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind—is that a logical, rational part of me knows it’s irrational. I have a graduate degree. I’ve been in IT for two decades. I know how redundant our systems are and I know that one guy missing one access rule usually doesn't bring down the global economy. I know it. But it absolutely does not matter what the logical part of my brain says. When that thought hits, it hits me so hard, like a physical blow, and it feels like I am entirely, personally responsible for preventing this massive disaster. It feels completely real in that moment. It's all on my shoulders. And because it feels so heavy and so terrifying, I can’t just brush it off. I can’t just say, \"Oh, that's just a silly thought,\" and go grab a cup of coffee. I just can't move on. I get completely paralyzed by it, trapped in that feeling of responsibility, which is why I end up doing all the checking and counting I told you about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: Values and beliefs... I mean, I guess I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that in this context. Like, if you're asking if I actually want those things to happen, or if I believe the company should fail, then God, no. Absolutely not. That's... that's the exact opposite of who I am. I've always been the guy who fixes things, you know? Ever since I started in this field, my whole identity, my whole... well, my belief system, I guess, has been about stability. Keeping the lights on. Making sure everything runs smoothly and safely. \n\nI've always prided myself on being the person who protects the network, the one who catches the mistakes before they happen. I take that responsibility incredibly seriously. So these thoughts, these horrible images of everything collapsing because of my negligence... they go against everything I stand for. It's like they are attacking the very core of what I care about. I mean, my entire career is built on being the reliable one, the manager who makes sure the infrastructure is rock solid. I'm a rational, pragmatic guy. I build systems that are supposed to make sense. \n\nSo having my brain constantly tell me I'm the one causing the disaster, that I'm the careless one who is going to ruin people's lives because I didn't check a log file enough times... it feels like a sick joke. It's completely contrary to the kind of person I've spent my entire life trying to be. It just doesn't fit who I am at all. It feels so alien. Does that make sense? Because I just... I don't understand why my mind would torture me with the exact opposite of what I actually value, and it just keeps looping and looping on the things that would devastate me the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your counting or checking behaviors are attempts to prevent the disasters you imagine from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I mean, that is exactly it. Like I was trying to explain earlier, it is entirely about stopping that disaster from happening. If I do not do these routines, I feel like I am basically pulling the trigger on the company's collapse myself. So, when I am sitting there at my desk—and I do this constantly—I will reread our deployment plans. And I do not mean just double-checking them like a normal manager would. I read them over and over and over again. I comb through the audit logs, line by line, and even dig through old messages just to make absolutely sure I did not miss some tiny, critical detail that is going to cause a massive data breach.\n\nAnd while I am doing all of this, I am counting. I am counting through the steps in my head, one, two, three, four, and I have to keep doing it, repeating the sequence, until my brain finally registers this specific safe feeling. If I do not reach that exact feeling of safety, I just cannot stop. That is why I end up checking the server room door locks repeatedly, just yanking on the handle to make sure it caught. \n\nLike I mentioned before with the security guard, I will literally stay at the office for hours after everyone else has packed up and gone home. The place is completely empty, the motion sensor lights are turning off in the hallways because nobody is around, and I am just stuck there, trapped, rereading logs or checking those locks. Because if I try to force myself to just grab my laptop bag and walk out to my car before the routine actually feels complete... the panic is just blinding. It is this overwhelming, suffocating dread. My chest gets tight, and my brain just screams at me that if I leave right then, the network will be compromised tonight, and it will be entirely my fault because I chose to walk away before finishing the check. I cannot live with that risk. So I just stay. I stay and I count and I check those messages and logs until the panic finally subsides enough for me to leave.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Enjoy? I... I don't even know what that word means right now, to be completely honest with you. It’s actually funny you ask that, well, not funny, but... tragic, really, because it's bleeding into absolutely every corner of my life. \n\nTake my evenings, for example. I used to have this really great group of guys I played pickup basketball with every Thursday night. Have for years. And salsa dancing, too—which I know, maybe it sounds a little out of character for a pragmatic IT guy, but I used to go to these salsa nights on the weekends. It was my release, you know? It was how I disconnected from the servers and the screens and just let myself exist in my body instead of my head. But I’ve just... I’ve completely stopped showing up for both of them. I haven't been in months. Because by the time the evening rolls around, I'm either physically trapped at the office doing my checking routines, or I'm so profoundly, mentally exhausted from the constant adrenaline and panic that I can't even fathom being around people. So I'm losing those routines. And worse, I'm losing the people there, too. They used to keep me grounded, they reminded me there's a whole world outside the network infrastructure, and now I've just completely isolated myself from them. \n\nBut it’s not even just the fun stuff, Doctor. That's the thing that really terrifies me. It's destroying the very thing I am trying so hard to protect—my actual job. We just had this massive cloud migration project. It was huge, months and months of planning, a critical transition for the company. And... I missed the major deadline. I just blew right past it. And it wasn't because I didn't know how to do it, it was because I literally couldn’t finish signing off on the final deployment decisions. Every time I looked at the approval forms, my brain would just scream at me that I had missed a vulnerability in the new architecture, that the second I signed my name, the data would be exposed. So I would go back and check the diagrams again, and read the logs again, and count my steps... and the deadline just came and went. \n\nDo you know how humiliating that is? For a senior manager to just freeze up like that? And now... my team, the engineers who are supposed to report to me and rely on me for direction... they’ve started working around me. I can see them doing it. They'll CC me on emails to be polite, but they won't wait for my approval anymore because they know I'll just become a bottleneck. They are actively bypassing me just to keep the department functioning. I'm trying so hard to save the company from disaster, and in the process, I'm making myself completely obsolete. It’s just... it’s taking everything from me. Everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about work disasters are consistent with your own identity and self-image?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was saying a minute ago when we were talking about my values... no. Not at all. It's completely contrary to my self-image. I see myself as a capable, decisive leader. But... well, the thing that actually brings me in to see you, the thing that is just breaking me down, is how these thoughts actually play out in my head and completely hijack that identity. \n\nI just keep getting these thoughts—these loud, inescapable, looping thoughts—that if I miss just one setting... one single access rule in the active directory, or... or even just one minor warning sign in a server log... that someone is going to get seriously hurt, or that the company and everyone who relies on us is going to lose absolutely everything. \n\nAnd the crazy part is, part of me knows it’s irrational. I mean, I have a graduate degree, I've built these architectures, I understand our fail-safes and redundancies better than anyone in the building! My logical brain knows that one minor misconfiguration usually doesn't bring down the entire grid. But when the thought hits me, all that logic just goes completely out the window. It hits me with this physical force, like I am personally, directly responsible for this massive catastrophe that hasn't even happened yet. \n\nAnd because it feels so real, so immediate, I can’t just move on. I can't just say to myself, 'Oh, that's a silly thought, you're a good manager,' and go back to drinking my coffee. I get completely paralyzed by it. The weight of that imagined responsibility just pins me to my desk. My whole self-image as a rational IT guy just evaporates, and I'm left feeling like this... this ticking time bomb of negligence. I try so hard to push past it, to just close the laptop and be the person I know I am, but the guilt of what *could* happen if I missed that one little access rule is so heavy that I just get stuck there. When that terrifying thought pops into my head, like we were just talking about, that thought that I’m a ticking time bomb and I’ve missed something critical... it’s so loud and so disturbing that I feel like I have to actively cancel it out. I have to somehow undo it in real time. \n\nSo, that’s when I start the routines I mentioned before. The building gets completely dark, the janitorial staff has already come and gone, and I'm just sitting there, completely exhausted, but I can't leave because the disturbing thought hasn't been fully undone yet. If I even try to pack up my laptop bag and stop the checking before the routine feels completely, one-hundred-percent complete... the panic just spikes to an unbearable level. It's like I'm leaving a literal bomb armed and walking away. I mean, yes, absolutely... I know I’m probably sounding like a broken record here, Doctor, because I feel like I’ve just been pouring all of this out over the last few minutes, but... the distress is just, it’s suffocating. It really is. 

When you ask about impairment, my mind just goes right back to that cloud migration deadline. I spent months on that project. I would sit there staring at the approval screens, and I just couldn't do it. I was paralyzed by the checking. And the sheer distress of watching that massive deadline blow right past us, knowing it was entirely my fault because I was stuck in my own head... it's agonizing. And now, exactly like I was saying, my team has just started working around me instead of relying on me. Do you know how incredibly distressing it is to be a manager, someone who worked hard for his degree and spent twenty years building a career, only to realize your own engineers are quietly bypassing you just to keep the department running? It’s humiliating. I sit in my office and I can practically feel them making decisions without me.

And the impairment outside of work is just as total. The basketball games, the salsa nights... I haven’t just missed a couple of weeks, I’ve completely stopped showing up for them. And it hurts. It really hurts, because I’m losing the routines and the people that used to keep me grounded. I used to be a guy who played point guard on Tuesdays and went out dancing on Thursdays, and now... I'm just watching myself disappear. I'm just a guy trapped at the office, exhausted, terrified of making a mistake. So, yes. The distress, the impairment... it’s absolute. I just don't know how much longer I can keep living like this.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, thank you for seeing me. Um... well, what brings me in is... it's my work, but it's not just my work, it's my head. I'm an information systems manager, I've been doing this a long time, got my master's degree, so I'm very used to complex environments. But it's gotten to the point where I keep getting these thoughts. Just these constant, heavy thoughts that if I miss one setting—just one tiny configuration on the servers—or if I overlook one access rule in the security protocols, or even just miss a subtle warning sign in the system logs... someone could get seriously hurt. \n\nOr, you know, people could lose everything. The company could go under, people could lose their livelihoods, their data, everything. And the frustrating thing is, part of me knows it’s irrational. I know we have redundancies built in, I know how the architecture works, I'm an educated guy. But it doesn't matter what the logical side of my brain says, because the thought just hits me so hard, like I’m personally responsible for every catastrophic thing that could possibly happen. I feel the weight of it like it's an absolute certainty. \n\nAnd I can’t just move on from it. I mean, most people would just close their laptop at five o'clock and go home to their families, right? But I can't do that. I just get stuck on that thought, over and over, playing out the disaster in my head, thinking about how it would all be my fault because of one missed line of code or one access rule, and it's just... it's exhausting. It's taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these thoughts about something going wrong at work come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yes. One hundred percent against my will. I mean, it's exactly like I was just saying... I don't sit down and choose to think about missing an access rule. I don't want to think about overlooking a warning sign in the system logs or missing a server setting. Who would want to think about that, right? \n\nBut it just... it just invades my brain. I'll be driving home, or maybe trying to sit down and watch a movie, and suddenly—bam. The thought just forces its way in. I'll try to push it away, I'll tell myself, you know, \"Hey, you know the architecture, you checked the redundancies, it's completely irrational to think this way.\" I try so hard to just push it out. But it doesn't matter what I want. The thought just parks itself right there in the front of my mind, and it brings this heavy, crushing feeling that someone is going to get hurt, or the company is going to lose everything, and it's going to be all my fault. \n\nIt's like my brain is hijacking itself. I just want to move on, you know? I want to just eat dinner or watch the game like a normal person, but I can't, because the thought just won't leave, no matter how hard I try to force it out or distract myself. It's completely without my permission, if that makes sense. It just... it takes over, and I'm just stuck there fighting with my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly doing anything or checking something in an attempt to make these thoughts go away or prevent the disasters you're imagining?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I do a lot of things. It's... well, it's embarrassing, to be honest, but since we're talking about it, I guess I should just lay it all out there. \n\nWhen those thoughts hit, the ones where I'm convinced I missed a setting and everything is going to crash, I can't just sit with it. I have to do something. So, I'll go back and reread the deployment plans. And I don't mean just skimming them to double-check a date or a version number. I mean I read them line by line, over and over again. I'll read the same paragraph five, six, seven times. And it's not just the plans... it's the audit logs, too. I'll scroll through lines of code and log entries, looking for that one tiny error I'm convinced I made. Even old messages—emails, Slack messages to my team from weeks ago—I'll dig them up and read them repeatedly to make sure I didn't give a wrong instruction that could lead to a catastrophe. \n\nAnd while I'm doing this, I... I have this counting thing. I'll count through the steps of the deployment or the security checks in my head. One, two, three, four... and it has to land on a \"safe\" number, or it has to feel a certain way. If I get distracted, or if the feeling isn't right—if it doesn't feel \"complete\" or \"safe\"—I have to start the whole counting process over. And I have to reread the document again. \n\nIt gets so bad, doctor, I've... I've stayed at the office for hours after everyone else has gone home. The lights are off in the rest of the building, the cleaning crew has come and gone, and I'm just sitting there at my desk, locked in this cycle. Because if I try to stop before that \"right\" feeling hits, if I try to just pack up my bag and walk out the door, the panic is unbearable. It physically hurts in my chest. \n\nAnd it's not even just the computer stuff anymore. It's bleeding into other things. When I finally do leave the office, I'll check the door locks. I'll lock the door, pull the handle, walk away, and then the thought hits—\"Did it actually latch? What if someone gets in and accesses the server room?\" So I go back. I pull the handle again. I'll do it ten, twenty times. I'll count the pulls. It's just... it's a nightmare. I'm spending hours doing this stuff just to make the thoughts stop, and it barely even works for more than a few minutes before the cycle starts all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking or counting behaviors to relieve the anxiety caused by your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That is exactly it. It’s entirely about the anxiety. I mean, like I was just saying a minute ago about rereading the deployment plans and the audit logs and all those old messages... That is exactly it. It’s entirely about the anxiety. I mean, like I was just saying a minute ago about rereading the deployment plans and the audit logs and all those old messages... I’m not doing any of that because I’m just trying to be a diligent manager, you know? I’m doing it because the anxiety is so intense, it’s like a physical weight sitting right on my chest. \n\nWhen that thought hits me—that I missed something, that the network is vulnerable and it’s going to ruin everything—my heart just starts pounding, my stomach drops, and the only way to make it stop, the only way to get even a tiny ounce of relief, is to do the checking. So I’ll sit there, and I’ll read the logs over and over, or I’ll be counting through the deployment steps in my head, just desperately praying to hit that 'safe' feeling. Because until I hit that feeling, the anxiety just keeps escalating. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same thing with the door locks. I’ll be standing there in the empty hallway, yanking on that handle, counting the pulls, because the panic is screaming at me that if I don't, the server room isn't secure and it'll be my fault. That’s exactly why I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves. It’s not because there’s actual work to do, or because I want to be there. God, I want to be home. But I’m trapped trying to get that anxiety to just drop, even a little bit. If I try to stop before it feels complete, before I get that exact right 'safe' feeling... the panic is just completely unbearable. It's like I'm suffocating. I can't even get to my car. So, yeah, I absolutely feel like I have to do it. I do all of it just to be able to breathe again, really.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's... it's destroying everything. I mean, completely upending my life. Just at work alone, for example, we had this massive cloud migration project. Huge deal for the company, a really critical infrastructure shift, and I was the manager in charge. But when it came time to actually execute, I... I missed the major deadline. I completely blew past it because I just couldn't finish signing off on the final architecture decisions. I’d sit there staring at the approval forms, and the panic would just take over, and I’d have to go back and check the redundancy protocols again, and then read the logs, and count the steps... well, you know, everything I just told you about. I couldn't bring myself to just put my name on it and say \"go.\" \n\nAnd because of that, my team... God, it’s humiliating, honestly. My own team has just started working around me. The people I’m supposed to be leading! They don't rely on me anymore. They’ll go to other directors or just quietly make the calls themselves because they know if they bring a decision to my desk, it’s going to get swallowed up in my endless checking loops. It makes me feel completely useless, like I'm just a ghost in my own department. \n\nAnd then, you know, you asked about outside of work... it's bleeding into all of that, too. I used to have a life. I really did. I had these routines that kept me grounded, kept me feeling like a normal, functioning human being. Like, I used to play in this pickup basketball game, and I’d go to these salsa dancing nights on the weekends. Which, I know, a 43-year-old IT guy doing salsa, it sounds a little funny maybe, but I loved it. It was my release. The music, the people, just moving and not thinking about servers or deployment schedules. \n\nBut I’ve just... I’ve completely stopped showing up. To all of it. I mean, how can I go play basketball when I’m stuck at the office for three extra hours yanking on a door handle? Or how can I be present and enjoy a dance when my brain is screaming at me that the whole company network is going to collapse and I'm going to be the one blamed for it? I can't. So I just stay home, or I stay at the office, and... I'm losing all of it. I'm losing the people I used to see, I'm losing the routines that kept me sane... I just don't even know who I am outside of this constant panic anymore. It just takes up every inch of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these counting or checking behaviors are necessary to neutralize or undo the thoughts that are disturbing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, it is a lot like what I was just trying to explain a minute ago, but it really is about... undoing it. It is like the thought itself is a virus, you know? Being an IT guy, I guess that is how I picture it. The thought pops into my head that I missed a security flaw, or that someone is going to breach the server room, and it literally feels like the disaster has already been set in motion just because the thought occurred to me. \n\nSo, yeah, when I am sitting there rereading those deployment plans, or combing through the audit logs and those old messages for the hundredth time... Like I said before, I will be counting through the steps in my head, and I have to reach that safe feeling. Because if a bad thought about the network crashing spikes while I am on step three, then step three is ruined. It is contaminated by the thought. I am pulling on that handle over and over to physically undo the image in my head of an intruder walking right in and destroying our hardware. But if I try to force myself to just stop before it feels complete, before the thought is actually neutralized... the panic is so absolute, it feels like I am intentionally choosing to let the company be destroyed. I just cannot walk away with that on my conscience, so I keep checking and counting until the thought is undone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about work and the potential disasters are a part of yourself or are they something that is being imposed on you from the outside?\n\nPatient: No, it’s... I know they’re my thoughts. I mean, I don't think anyone is beaming them into my head or anything like that, if that's what you're asking. They are definitely coming from my own brain. But at the same time, it almost feels like my brain has been hijacked by this worst-case-scenario generator that I can't turn off. \n\nHonestly, that’s really what brings me in here to see you. And it goes beyond just getting fired or the company losing money. My mind takes it to the absolute extreme, telling me that because of my mistake, someone could actually get hurt, or our clients could lose everything they've built because their data was compromised. \n\nAnd the most frustrating part of all of this—the part that makes me feel like I'm losing my mind—is that a logical, rational part of me knows it’s irrational. I have a graduate degree. I’ve been in IT for two decades. I know how redundant our systems are and I know that one guy missing one access rule usually doesn't bring down the global economy. I know it. But it absolutely does not matter what the logical part of my brain says. When that thought hits, it hits me so hard, like a physical blow, and it feels like I am entirely, personally responsible for preventing this massive disaster. It feels completely real in that moment. It's all on my shoulders. And because it feels so heavy and so terrifying, I can’t just brush it off. I can’t just say, \"Oh, that's just a silly thought,\" and go grab a cup of coffee. I just can't move on. I don't understand why my mind would torture me with the exact opposite of what I actually value, and it just keeps looping and looping on the things that would devastate me the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your counting or checking behaviors are attempts to prevent the disasters you imagine from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I mean, that is exactly it. Like I was trying to explain earlier, it is entirely about stopping that disaster from happening. I read them over and over and over again. It is this overwhelming, suffocating dread. My chest gets tight, and my brain just screams at me that if I leave right then, the network will be compromised tonight, and it will be entirely my fault because I chose to walk away before finishing the check. I cannot live with that risk. So I just stay. I get completely paralyzed by it, trapped in that feeling of responsibility, which is why I end up doing all the checking and counting I told you about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: Values and beliefs... I mean, I guess I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that in this context. Like, if you're asking if I actually want those things to happen, or if I believe the company should fail, then God, no. Absolutely not. That's... that's the exact opposite of who I am. I've always been the guy who fixes things, you know? Ever since I started in this field, my whole identity, my whole... well, my belief system, I guess, has been about stability. Keeping the lights on. Making sure everything runs smoothly and safely. \n\nI've always prided myself on being the person who protects the network, the one who catches the mistakes before they happen. I take that responsibility incredibly seriously. So these thoughts, these horrible images of everything collapsing because of my negligence... they go against everything I stand for. It's like they are attacking the very core of what I care about. I mean, my entire career is built on being the reliable one, the manager who makes sure the infrastructure is rock solid. I'm a rational, pragmatic guy. I build systems that are supposed to make sense. \n\nSo having my brain constantly tell me I'm the one causing the disaster, that I'm the careless one who is going to ruin people's lives because I didn't check a log file enough times... it feels like a sick joke. It's completely contrary to the kind of person I've spent my entire life trying to be. It just doesn't fit who I am at all. It feels so alien. Does that make sense? Because I just... I don't understand why my mind would torture me with the exact opposite of what I actually value, and it just keeps looping and looping on the things that would devastate me the most.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your counting or checking behaviors are attempts to prevent the disasters you imagine from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I mean, that is exactly it. Like I was trying to explain earlier, it is entirely about stopping that disaster from happening. If I do not do these routines, I feel like I am basically pulling the trigger on the company's collapse myself. So, when I am sitting there at my desk—and I do this constantly—I will reread our deployment plans. And I do not mean just double-checking them like a normal manager would. I read them over and over and over again. I comb through the audit logs, line by line, and even dig through old messages just to make absolutely sure I did not miss some tiny, critical detail that is going to cause a massive data breach.\n\nAnd while I am doing all of this, I am counting. I am counting through the steps in my head, one, two, three, four, and I have to keep doing it, repeating the sequence, until my brain finally registers this specific safe feeling. If I do not reach that exact feeling of safety, I just cannot stop. That is why I end up checking the server room door locks repeatedly, just yanking on the handle to make sure it caught. \n\nLike I mentioned before with the security guard, I will literally stay at the office for hours after everyone else has packed up and gone home. The place is completely empty, the motion sensor lights are turning off in the hallways because nobody is around, and I am just stuck there, trapped, rereading logs or checking those locks. Because if I try to force myself to just grab my laptop bag and walk out to my car before the routine actually feels complete... the panic is just blinding. It is this overwhelming, suffocating dread. My chest gets tight, and my brain just screams at me that if I leave right then, the network will be compromised tonight, and it will be entirely my fault because I chose to walk away before finishing the check. I cannot live with that risk. So I just stay. I stay and I count and I check those messages and logs until the panic finally subsides enough for me to leave.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Enjoy? I... I don't even know what that word means right now, to be completely honest with you. It’s actually funny you ask that, well, not funny, but... tragic, really, because it's bleeding into absolutely every corner of my life. \n\nTake my evenings, for example. I used to have this really great group of guys I played pickup basketball with every Thursday night. Have for years. And salsa dancing, too—which I know, maybe it sounds a little out of character for a pragmatic IT guy, but I used to go to these salsa nights on the weekends. It was my release, you know? It was how I disconnected from the servers and the screens and just let myself exist in my body instead of my head. But I’ve just... I’ve completely stopped showing up for both of them. I haven't been in months. Because by the time the evening rolls around, I'm either physically trapped at the office doing my checking routines, or I'm so profoundly, mentally exhausted from the constant adrenaline and panic that I can't even fathom being around people. So I'm losing those routines. And worse, I'm losing the people there, too. They used to keep me grounded, they reminded me there's a whole world outside the network infrastructure, and now I've just completely isolated myself from them. \n\nBut it’s not even just the fun stuff, Doctor. That's the thing that really terrifies me. It's destroying the very thing I am trying so hard to protect—my actual job. We just had this massive cloud migration project. It was huge, months and months of planning, a critical transition for the company. And... I missed the major deadline. I just blew right past it. And it wasn't because I didn't know how to do it, it was because I literally couldn’t finish signing off on the final deployment decisions. Every time I looked at the approval forms, my brain would just scream at me that I had missed a vulnerability in the new architecture, that the second I signed my name, the data would be exposed. So I would go back and check the diagrams again, and read the logs again, and count my steps... and the deadline just came and went. \n\nDo you know how humiliating that is? For a senior manager to just freeze up like that? And now... my team, the engineers who are supposed to report to me and rely on me for direction... they’ve started working around me. I can see them doing it. They'll CC me on emails to be polite, but they won't wait for my approval anymore because they know I'll just become a bottleneck. They are actively bypassing me just to keep the department functioning. I'm trying so hard to save the company from disaster, and in the process, I'm making myself completely obsolete. It’s just... it’s taking everything from me. Everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about work disasters are consistent with your own identity and self-image?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was saying a minute ago when we were talking about my values... no. Not at all. It's completely contrary to my self-image. I see myself as a capable, decisive leader. But... well, the thing that actually brings me in to see you, the thing that is just breaking me down, is how these thoughts actually play out in my head and completely hijack that identity. \n\nI just keep getting these thoughts—these loud, inescapable, looping thoughts—that if I miss just one setting... one single access rule in the active directory, or... or even just one minor warning sign in a server log... that someone is going to get seriously hurt, or that the company and everyone who relies on us is going to lose absolutely everything. \n\nAnd the crazy part is, part of me knows it’s irrational. I mean, I have a graduate degree, I've built these architectures, I understand our fail-safes and redundancies better than anyone in the building! My logical brain knows that one minor misconfiguration usually doesn't bring down the entire grid. But when the thought hits me, all that logic just goes completely out the window. It hits me with this physical force, like I am personally, directly responsible for this massive catastrophe that hasn't even happened yet. \n\nAnd because it feels so real, so immediate, I can’t just move on. I can't just say to myself, 'Oh, that's a silly thought, you're a good manager,' and go back to drinking my coffee. I get completely paralyzed by it. I try so hard to push past it, to just close the laptop and be the person I know I am, but the guilt of what *could* happen if I missed that one little access rule is so heavy that I just get stuck there. I can't move past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your counting or checking behaviors are attempts to neutralize or undo the thoughts that are disturbing you?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. When that terrifying thought pops into my head, like we were just talking about, that thought that I’m a ticking time bomb and I’ve missed something critical... it’s so loud and so disturbing that I feel like I have to actively cancel it out. I have to somehow undo it in real time. \n\nSo, that’s when I start the routines I mentioned before. I'll just be sitting there, counting the steps, over and over, trying to force my brain to finally register that specific 'safe' feeling. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with the physical security, like the door locks in the server room.
